Complete Diagrammatic Axiomatisations of Relative Entropy Volume: NN Journal: Electronic Notes in Theoretical Informatics and Computer Science Volume: NN
Ralph Sarkis a, Fabio Zanasi a

Abstract
Relative entropy is a fundamental class of distances between probability distributions, with widespread applications in probability theory, statistics, and machine learning. In this work, we study relative entropy from a categorical perspective, viewing it as a quantitative enrichment of categories of stochastic matrices. We consider two natural monoidal structures on stochastic matrices, given by the Kronecker product and the direct sum. Our main results are complete axiomatisations of Kullback–Leibler divergence and, more generally, of Rényi divergences of arbitrary order, for each such structure. Our axiomatic theories are formulated within the framework of quantitative monoidal algebra, using a graphical language of string diagrams enriched with quantitative equations.



keywords

string diagram, complete axiomatisation, categorical semantics, relative entropy, Kullback–Leibler divergence, Rényi divergence

1
Introduction

Programming language semantics traditionally investigates when two programs are
equivalent
with respect to a given notion of observation, or whether a program satisfies properties such as correctness, termination, or safety. When programs exhibit random behaviour, however—as in probabilistic programming, statistical inference, and machine learning—equivalence is often too coarse to be practically informative. Rather than asking whether two programs produce identical outputs, it is more useful to measure
how far apart
their behaviours are. This shift in perspective has led to extensive research on behavioural distances and program metrics (​​
[
19
,
65
,
16
,
7
,
18
]
).

A prominent line of work in this direction is the programme of
quantitative algebraic theories
, initiated by Mardare et al. (​​
[
45
]
). This framework provides axiomatic presentations of (pseudo)metrics via calculi whose judgements have the form
s=_{\varepsilon}t
, expressing that the distance between terms
s
and
t
is at most
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
. Subsequent developments have extended the framework to Markov processes (​​
[
2
,
3
]
), higher-order settings (​​
[
17
]
), quantitative logics (​​
[
4
,
5
]
), string diagrams (​​
[
41
]
), and enriched algebraic theories (​​
[
56
,
57
]
).

Complete axiomatisations for the Kantorovich metric and total variation distance, introduced in
[
45
]
, are chief examples of quantitative algebraic theories. In this paper, we focus on another fundamental class of distances between probability distributions: relative entropy. In particular, Rényi divergences (​​
[
55
]
), which include the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence (​​
[
37
]
), play a central role across mathematics and computer science, with applications in statistical inference and machine learning (​​
[
22
,
36
,
28
]
), differential privacy (​​
[
21
,
48
]
), information geometry (​​
[
49
]
), and program semantics (​​
[
10
,
59
]
). Unlike the Kantorovich metric and total variation distance, however, no complete quantitative algebraic theory has yet been developed for KL divergence or, more generally, for relative entropies. Our work fills that gap.

Following
[
41
]
, we formulate our results at the level of symmetric monoidal categories, presenting our axiomatic calculi in the graphical language of
string diagrams
within the framework of
monoidal algebra
(​​
[
60
,
53
]
). This choice is motivated mainly by two considerations. First, it allows us to reason axiomatically about distances between
multi-dimensional
systems, such as stochastic matrices, rather than merely probability distributions. Indeed, stochastic matrices do not form a cartesian category; instead, they assemble into a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{FStoch}
, which supports a rich analysis of Bayesian inference, causality, and probabilistic graphical models (​​
[
31
,
42
,
43
]
). Second, it allows us to build on a substantial body of work on diagrammatic axiomatisations of probabilistic computation (particularly
[
24
,
52
]
, but
cf.
also
[
25
,
62
,
64
]
) and to extend it in a quantitative direction.

We shall study two enrichments of
\mathsf{FStoch}
with KL divergence, distinguished by their monoidal structure. One uses the Kronecker product of matrices, denoted
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
, and the other the direct sum, denoted
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
. Both structures are natural and useful in different contexts. The category
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
provides the standard setting for synthetic probability theory (​​
[
25
]
) and for applications to Bayesian networks and causal reasoning (​​
[
31
,
42
,
43
]
). In contrast,
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
is closely related to convex sets and barycentric algebras, which have been extensively studied since at least the ’50s (​​
[
63
]
), and to the interpretation of randomness as a monadic effect (see e.g.
[
14
,
47
,
46
]
).
Our main contributions are as follows.

(1)

KL divergence.
We provide axiomatisations for KL divergence enrichments of
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
(the restriction of
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
to objects of the form
2^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
)
1
1

1

This restriction is due to the fact that non-enriched
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
has been axiomatised, in
[
64
]
, but no analogous result holds for the full
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
.
and of
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
(
definitions

4.1
and
4.8
), and show their completeness (
theorems

4.6
and
4.13
).
Concretely, we present quantitative string diagrammatic theories whose freely generated enriched SMCs are isomorphic to enriched
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
, respectively. A key ingredient in our axiomatisations is the chain rule, which decomposes the divergence between joint distributions in terms of the divergences between their conditionals. We express this decomposition as a quantitative implication: the premises bound the distances between conditionals, while the conclusion bounds the distance between the corresponding joint distributions. Two such implications,
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
and
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
, appear in the axiom systems for
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
, respectively, and play a central role in the completeness proofs (
lemmas

4.4
and
4.11
).

(2)

Rényi divergences.
With minor adaptations, we show that our method extends to an axiomatisation of Rényi divergences of arbitrary order
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
, again for both monoidal structures. KL divergence arises as the special case
\alpha=1
.

(3)

Implicational quantitative diagrammatic reasoning.
To state our results, we need to extend the notion of quantitative monoidal theory introduced in
[
41
]
to allow axioms formulated as implications between quantitative equations. This mirrors the original formulation of quantitative equational reasoning in a cartesian setting (​​
[
45
]
), but now considered in the monoidal setting. The resulting framework is of independent interest: implicational axioms arise, for example, in the study of uniform traces (​​
[
29
,
12
]
) and balanced Markov categories (​​
[
38
,
23
]
).

Related work.
As noted above, quantitative algebraic theories provide the broader context for our results. Since
(\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus})^{\mathrm{op}}
is cartesian, our axiomatisation could in principle yield an analogous result within the original framework of
[
45
]
, which is based on classical cartesian algebra (we return to this observation in the conclusions). However, this is not the case for
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
(and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
), which therefore requires the more general setting of monoidal algebra.

Our work builds directly on
[
41
]
, where
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
enriched with total variation distance is axiomatised. A different diagrammatic axiomatisation of the same enrichment for
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
appears in
[
20
]
, albeit outside the formal framework of
[
41
]
. By introducing implicational axioms for diagrammatic calculi, we reconcile these approaches and bring
[
20
]
within the scope of quantitative monoidal algebra.

KL divergence originates in Shannon’s information theory (​​
[
61
]
) and has been axiomatised multiple times using functional equations (​​
[
30
,
34
,
50
]
). More recently, categorical approaches have led to novel and simpler characterisations (​​
[
6
,
26
,
27
]
). In these works, a category of stochastic processes based on
\mathsf{FStoch}
is defined, and it is shown that any functor satisfying a small set of natural postulates must compute KL divergence. Related ideas also yield a concise non-categorical axiomatisation in
[
40
]
. Perrone (​​
[
51
]
) proves that relative entropy defines an enrichment of
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
, without characterising it. Our results provide precisely such a characterisation.

Synopsis.

section

2
recalls necessary background on string diagrams, quantales, enriched categories, and relative entropy.
section

3
presents our extension of quantitative monoidal theories with implicational axioms. We define the logical system, describe how it freely generates syntactic categories, and establish its soundness and completeness with respect to models in enriched monoidal categories.
section

4
develops the axiomatisations of KL divergence for
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
.
section

5
extends these results to the full family of Rényi divergences. We conclude with a discussion of related and future work.

2
Background

2.1
Monoidal Algebra

We assume familiarity with basic category theory and in particular with monoidal categories (see e.g.
[
44
]
). We recall notions in monoidal algebra, the study of algebraic structures borne in monoidal categories. A detailed account can be found in
[
53
]
. In the sequel, SMC is short for symmetric strict monoidal category.

Definition 2.1
.

A
monoidal signature
is the data of a set
\Sigma_{0}
of
sorts
, a set
\Sigma_{1}
of
generators
, and two maps
\mathsf{in},\mathsf{out}:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{0}^{*}
assigning to each generator
f
a finite list of sorts for its
inputs
and for its
outputs
. We write
f:u\rightarrow v\in\Sigma_{1}
to mean that
f
is a generator with inputs
\mathsf{in}(f)=u
and outputs
\mathsf{out}(f)=v
. We often write
\Sigma
to refer to the tuple
(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{in},\mathsf{out})
.

Given a monoidal signature
\Sigma
, let
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
denote the set of all monoidal terms built out of generators in
\Sigma
with the following rules:

f:u\rightarrow v\in\Sigma_{1}

Gen

f:u\rightarrow v\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

x,y\in\Sigma_{0}

Swap

\sigma_{x,y}\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

x\in\Sigma_{0}

id

\mathrm{id}_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

Unit

\mathrm{id}\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

f:u\rightarrow v\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

g:v\rightarrow w\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

Seq

f\mathbin{;}g:u\rightarrow w\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

f:u\rightarrow v\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

f:u^{\prime}\rightarrow v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

Par

f\otimes f^{\prime}:uu^{\prime}\rightarrow vv^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}

Throughout the paper, we represent monoidal terms with string diagrams: a term
f:u\rightarrow v\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
is drawn as
u
f
v
, and the rules above are depicted as follows. We often omit input and output labels.

\mathbin{;}
and

) containing the equations in

. Intuitively, deformations that do not break strings or change the order of inputs and outputs do not alter the meaning of the picture. String diagrams assemble into an SMC, also denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
. Its objects are finite lists of sorts in
\Sigma_{0}
, morphisms
u\rightarrow v
are string diagrams
u
f

, composition is described by
Seq
, and monoidal product is concatenation on objects and
Par
on morphisms.

Figure 1
:
Laws of symmetric monoidal categories. Dotted boxes denote bracketing that is later omitted thanks to the laws.

Definition 2.2
.

A
monoidal theory

\mathscr{T}
comprises a signature
\Sigma
and a set
E
of
axioms
, which are pairs of monoidal terms
f,g\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
, with matching inputs and outputs, denoted
f=g
to convey their interpretation. A
model
of
\mathscr{T}
is an SMC
\mathbf{C}
equipped with a symmetric monoidal functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, such that
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
for all
f=g\in E
. Every such model factors uniquely through the
syntactic category

\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}
built as a quotient of
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
by the smallest congruence containing the axioms in
E
.

A recurring goal in monoidal algebra is the axiomatisation of an SMC
\mathbf{C}
with a monoidal theory, that is, to find
\mathscr{T}=(\Sigma,E)
and a model
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
that factors through an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}\cong\mathbf{C}
. We say that
\mathscr{T}

presents

\mathbf{C}
. Our main results are based on existing axiomatisations of SMCs of stochastic matrices.

Example 2.3
.

The category
\mathsf{FStoch}
has natural numbers as objects, and as morphisms
n\rightarrow m
, the
m\times n
stochastic matrices (matrices with entries in
[0,1]
whose columns each sum up to
1
). When
n
or
m
are zero, there is a unique empty stochastic matrix
[]
of type
n\rightarrow m
. Composition is by matrix multiplication and identity morphisms are matrices with
1
on every entry of the diagonal.

We consider two different monoidal products on stochastic matrices: the Kronecker product, denoted
\otimes
, and the direct sum, denoted
\oplus
, defined below in block matrix form with their units and symmetries.

A\otimes B\coloneq\scalebox{0.7}{$\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}B&\dots&a_{1n}B\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{m1}B&\dots&a_{mn}B\end{bmatrix}$}\quad I^{\otimes}\coloneq 1\quad\sigma_{n,m}^{\otimes}\coloneq\scalebox{0.7}{$\begin{bmatrix}E_{11}&\dots&E_{1n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
E_{m1}&\dots&E_{mn}\end{bmatrix}$}\qquad A\oplus B\coloneq\scalebox{0.7}{$\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}$}\quad I^{\oplus}\coloneq 0\quad\sigma_{n,m}^{\oplus}\coloneq\scalebox{0.7}{$\begin{bmatrix}0&I_{m}\\
I_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}$},

where
E_{ij}
is the
n\times m
matrix with a single
1
at column
i
, row
j
, and
I_{n}
is the identity
n\times n
matrix. Note that
\otimes
acts by multiplication on objects, while
\oplus
acts by addition.

These form the SMCs
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
. Only the latter has been fully axiomatised with a monoidal theory, but there is an axiomatisation of a full subcategory of
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
:
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
, the SMC of stochastic matrices of dimensions that are powers of
2
. Its objects are still natural numbers,
\mathsf{BStoch}(n,m)=\mathsf{FStoch}(2^{n},2^{m})
, and
\otimes
is still the Kronecker product, but it acts by addition on objects (since
2^{n+m}=2^{n}2^{m}
). We briefly recall the monoidal theories
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
and
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
that present
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
respectively.

The monoidal theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
(standing for
causal circuits
) has generators

,

, and

p

for each
p\in[0,1]
, and axioms as in
fig.

2
below. We write
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
for its syntactic category.

(2)

2.2
Quantale-Valued Relations

While our main applications are about axiomatising real-valued distances, our theoretical framework is presented in more general terms with quantale-valued relations.

Definition 2.4
.

A
quantale

\mathcal{V}
consists of a set
\mathcal{V}
equipped with a partial order
\sqsubseteq
and a binary operation
+:\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
satisfying the following properties:

•

any subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
has a supremum
\bigsqcup S
and an infimum
\bigsqcap S
, so that
(\mathcal{V},\sqsubseteq)
is a
complete lattice
. In particular,
\mathcal{V}
has a
bottom element

\bot=\bigsqcup\emptyset=\bigsqcap\mathcal{V}
and a
top element

\top=\bigsqcup\mathcal{V}=\bigsqcap\emptyset
;

•

+
is
associative
, namely, for any
x,y,z\in S
,
x+(y+z)=(x+y)+z
; and

•

+

preserves supremums
, namely, for any
x\in\mathcal{V}
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
x+\bigsqcup S=\bigsqcup\left\{x+a\mid a\in S\right\}
and
\bigsqcup S+x=\bigsqcup\left\{a+x\mid a\in S\right\}
.

We say that
\mathcal{V}
is
unital
if it contains an element
k\in\mathcal{V}
that is neutral for
+
(
x+k=x=k+x
for all
x\in\mathcal{V}
).
In this work, we will assume that quantales are unital. The main example we consider is the extended nonnegative reals, also called Lawvere’s quantale (​​
[
39
]
).

Example 2.5
.

Nonnegative real numbers with
\infty
form a complete lattice. By defining addition with infinity as
x+\infty=\infty
for all
x\in[0,\infty]
, we obtain an associative operation that preserves infimums. Thus,
[0,\infty]
with the opposite order
\geq
and addition forms a quantale with unit
0
, denoted
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-relation
is a set
X
equipped with a function
d:X\times X\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
, often denoted as a pair
(X,d)
. A morphism between
\mathcal{V}
-relations
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
is a function
f:X\rightarrow Y
satisfying, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\sqsubseteq d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))
. We denote by
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
the category of
\mathcal{V}
-relations, where identities and compositions are as for functions.

An isomorphism between
\mathcal{V}
-relations
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
is a function
f:X\rightarrow Y
that is a bijection and satisfies, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})=d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))
.

Example 2.7
.

A
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-relation
(X,d)
is often thought of as a set equipped with a
distance
. For
x,y\in X
,
d(x,y)
is a number that could represent the physical distance, or time, or amount of work, required to go from
x
to
y
. A morphism of
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-relations is called a
nonexpansive map
because
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\geq d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))
is interpreted as
f
mapping elements closer together (not expanding the space). An isomorphism of
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-relations is also called an
isometry
.

The work on quantitative algebraic reasoning initiated in
[
45
]
relies on representing distances as families of binary predicates. More precisely, given
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{V}
, the
equality up to
\varepsilon
predicate on a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
(X,d)
, denoted by
=_{\varepsilon}
, is defined as
x=_{\varepsilon}y\Leftrightarrow d(x,y)\sqsupseteq\varepsilon
. For
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
, we read
x=_{\varepsilon}y
as saying that the distance from
x
to
y
is less than
\varepsilon
. These predicates determine
d
, which enables reasoning about
\mathcal{V}
-relations in a logical system using these predicates, after enforcing monotonicity and continuity conditions.

Proposition 2.8
.

​​​​
[
58
, Proposition 2.21]

There is a correspondence between
\mathcal{V}
-relations on a set
X
and families of relations
{=_{\varepsilon}}\subseteq X\times X
, indexed by
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{V}
, that are
monotone
(if
\varepsilon\sqsupseteq\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
x=_{\varepsilon}y
then
x=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}y
) and
continuous
(if
x=_{\varepsilon_{i}}y
for all
i
in some index set
I
, then
x=_{\sqcup_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}y
).

The category
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
has categorical products that lift cartesian products of sets. Given two
\mathcal{V}
-relations
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
, the
\mathcal{V}
-relation on
X\times Y
defined by
d((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))=d_{X}(x,y)\sqcap d_{Y}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
satisfies the universal property of the product. This defines a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
, which we denote by
\mathbin{\boxtimes_{\sqcap}}
. Its unit is the reflexive
\mathcal{V}
-relation on a singleton,
\mathbf{1}
.
We may consider categories enriched in
(\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel},\mathbin{\boxtimes_{\sqcap}},\mathbf{1})
, but in the following section, we will use another monoidal structure.

Given
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
, there is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation on
X\times Y
defined by
d((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))=d_{X}(x,y)+d_{Y}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. When
\mathcal{V}
is commutative (i.e.
a+b=b+a
), this also defines a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
, which we denote by
\mathbin{\boxtimes_{+}}
. Its unit is also
\mathbf{1}
. In the sequel, we assume
\mathcal{V}
is commutative.

2.3
Categories Enriched over
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}

Enriched
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-categories enjoy a straightforward concrete characterisation that will be more convenient to us than the abstract definition of enrichment in e.g.
[
35
]
. Indeed, a
(\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel},\mathbin{\boxtimes_{+}},\mathbf{1})
-enriched category
\mathbf{C}
is simply a category
\mathbf{C}
equipped with a
\mathcal{V}
-relation on each of its homsets where composition is a morphism of
\mathcal{V}
-relations. Unrolled: for every objects
X,Y\in\mathbf{C}
, there is a function
d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X,Y}:\mathbf{C}(X,Y)\times\mathbf{C}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
, we often simply write
d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}
or
d
, and for every morphisms
f,f^{\prime}:X\rightarrow Y
and
g,g^{\prime}:Y\rightarrow Z
in
\mathbf{C}
,

d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X,Z}(f\mathbin{;}g,f^{\prime}\mathbin{;}g^{\prime})\sqsupseteq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X,Y}(f,f^{\prime})+d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{Y,Z}(g,g^{\prime}).

(3)

We can further impose on a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-category that it is monoidally enriched, or that it is an enriched monoidal category, meaning that it has a monoidal structure compatible with the enrichment. Concretely, if
(\mathbf{C},\otimes,I)
is a symmetric monoidal category and
\mathbf{C}
is enriched over
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
, then we say it is
monoidally enriched
if
\otimes
is a morphism of
\mathcal{V}
-relations, i.e. for all
f,f^{\prime}:X\rightarrow Y
and
g,g^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,

d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X\otimes X^{\prime},Y\otimes Y^{\prime}}(f\otimes g,f^{\prime}\otimes g^{\prime})\sqsupseteq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X,Y}(f,f^{\prime})+d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}(g,g^{\prime}).

(4)

We will call such a category a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC
.

We also mention that
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-enriched functors are functors between the underlying categories that are locally morphisms of
\mathcal{V}
-relations, that is,
F:\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
is enriched whenever
d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}(f,g)\sqsubseteq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{D}}(F(f),F(g))
for all morphisms
f,g\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
. Similarly, an enriched isomorphism is an isomorphism between the underlying categories that is locally an isomorphism of
\mathcal{V}
-relations.

In case
\mathcal{V}={[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
, we say an enriched functor is
locally nonexpansive
and an enriched isomorphism is
locally an isometry
.

2.4
Relative Entropy

Let
\mathcal{D}X
denote the set of
finitely supported distributions
over a set
X
, namely,

\mathcal{D}X\coloneq\{\varphi:X\rightarrow[0,1]\mid\varphi^{-1}(0,1]\text{ is finite and }\textstyle\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)=1\}.

We write
\mathord{\lvert x\rangle}\in\mathcal{D}X
for the
Dirac distribution
at
x\in X
defined by
\mathord{\lvert x\rangle}(x^{\prime})=0
if
x\neq x^{\prime}
and
\mathord{\lvert x\rangle}(x)=1
.

Relative entropy, also called Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence (​​
[
37
]
), is a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-relation on
\mathcal{D}X
.

Definition 2.9
.

KL divergence
is a map
\mathcal{D}X\times\mathcal{D}X\rightarrow{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
defined by
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)\coloneq\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)\log\frac{\varphi(x)}{\psi(x)}
, with
\log\frac{x}{0}=\infty
and
0\cdot\infty=0
as conventions. In particular,
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)=\infty
if and only if there exists some
x\in X
such that
\varphi(x)>0=\psi(x)
, and
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\varphi)=0
.

Example 2.10
.

For any natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\underline{n}\coloneq\{1,\dots,n\}
. We can represent a distribution in
\mathcal{D}\underline{n}
as a vector in
[0,1]^{n}
whose entries sum up to
1
. Then, the KL divergence between two distributions is

\mathsf{kl}([a_{1},\dots,a_{n}],[b_{1},\dots,b_{n}])=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\log\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}.

The following instance, with
n=2
, will appear several times in the rest of the paper.

\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})=p\log\frac{p}{q}+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}.

Note that if
q\in\{0,1\}
and
p\neq q
, then
\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})=\infty
. If
p=q
, then
\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.45}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}})=0
.

A renowned property of KL divergence, called the
chain rule
(​​
[
54
, Theorem 2.15]
), allows to decompose the computation of the divergence for distributions over products or coproducts of sets. We state two specific instances of the chain rule that will be key in the axiomatisations of
sections

4.2
and
4.3
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
Y\neq\emptyset
be a set,
\mathbb{B}\coloneq\{\mathtt{0},\mathtt{1}\}
,
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}\times Y)
,
p\coloneq\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{1},y)
, and
q\coloneq\sum_{y\in Y}\psi(\mathtt{1},y)
. Writing
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)\coloneq y\mapsto\nicefrac{{\varphi(x,y)}}{{p}}
and
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)\coloneq y\mapsto\nicefrac{{\varphi(x,y)}}{{1-p}}
for the conditional distributions on
Y
,
2
2

2

If
p=0
(resp.
p=1
)
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)
(resp.
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)
) is assumed to be an arbitrary distribution on
Y
.
and similarly for
\overline{\psi}
, we have

\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))+(1-p)\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-)).

(5)

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
X,Y\neq\emptyset
be sets,
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(X+Y)
,
p\coloneq\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)
and
q\coloneq\sum_{x\in X}\psi(x)
. Writing
\varphi_{X}\coloneq x\mapsto\varphi(x)/p
for the conditional distribution on
X
, and similarly for
\varphi_{Y}
,
\psi_{X}
, and
\psi_{Y}
, we have

\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})+(1-p)\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y}).

(6)

We remark that, in both cases,
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)
is a monotone function of the divergences between the conditional distributions. More precisely, looking at
eq.

5
, if you bound
\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-))
from above, then you get an upper bound for
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)
, and similarly for
eq.

6
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))\leq\varepsilon\text{ and }\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-))\leq\delta

\displaystyle\implies\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)\leq\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\varepsilon+(1-p)\delta

(7)

\displaystyle\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})\leq\varepsilon\text{ and }\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y})\leq\delta

\displaystyle\implies\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)\leq\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\varepsilon+(1-p)\delta.

(8)

This is particularly relevant for us because our logical framework does not reason about exact distance. We use the predicates
=_{\varepsilon}
which are interpreted as upper bounds on the distance by
\varepsilon\in{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
.

3
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-Enriched Monoidal Theories

The framework of quantitative monoidal algebra, as developed in
[
41
]
, only allows for inference rules describing the quantitative behaviour of sequential composition and parallel composition of string diagrams (on top of the usual pseudometric axioms like triangle inequality). In order to axiomatise relative entropy in
sections

4
and
5
below, we need a more general setting, allowing to model the chain rule as an inference rule of our diagrammatic theory. For this purpose, we now develop an extension of quantitative monoidal algebra, which allows the formation of inference rules not necessarily restricted to sequential and parallel composition of string diagrams. Besides contributing to our later results, this study is of interest for other works on quantitative algebra, as discussed in Section
6
.

3.1
Quantitative Implications

Let
\Sigma
be a monoidal signature and
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
be its set of terms. We define
quantitative implications
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-equation
between terms is a pair of terms
(s,t)
in
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
along with an element
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{V}
, which we shall write
s=_{\varepsilon}t
.
A
\mathcal{V}
-implication
consists of a set
\Gamma
of (
\mathcal{V}
-)equations called the
premises
along with a (
\mathcal{V}
-)equation
\varphi
called the
conclusion
, which we will write as
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
.

Given a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC
\mathbf{C}
and a symmetric monoidal functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, we define
satisfaction
. An equation
f=g
is satisfied when
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
. A
\mathcal{V}
-equation
f=_{\varepsilon}g
is satisfied when
d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\sqsupseteq\varepsilon
. A
\mathcal{V}
-implication
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
is satisfied when either
\varphi
is satisfied or at least one (
\mathcal{V}
)-equation in
\Gamma
is not satisfied.

Oftentimes, we write an expression that is interpreted as a family of
\mathcal{V}
-implications. For instance,
we can write the following
\mathcal{V}
-implications that are always satisfied in
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMCs by
proposition

2.8
.

f
=_{\varepsilon}
g
\Rightarrow
f
=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
g

\displaystyle\forall\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}\text{ such that }\varepsilon\sqsupseteq\varepsilon^{\prime}

(9)

f
=_{\varepsilon_{i}}
g
\Rightarrow
f
=_{\sqcup_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}
g
\forall i
\big(
\big)

\displaystyle\forall\left\{\varepsilon_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{V}

(10)

There is an explicit quantification over elements in
\mathcal{V}
, but there is another implicit quantification of
f
and
g
:

f
and
g
are
metavariables
, and we interpret these expressions as families of
\mathcal{V}
-implications where
f
and
g
are replaced by every possible terms that typecheck (e.g.
f
and
g
must have the same inputs and outputs to be comparable in
eqs.

9
and
10
).

In the sequel, we will denote metavariables with symbols that are not defined in the scope to implicitly imply that they are universally quantified. We often write
f
,
g
,
h
, etc. for generic terms, and
\varepsilon
,
\delta
, etc. for generic elements of
\mathcal{V}
.

Example 3.2
.

Let us continue the list of
\mathcal{V}
-implications that are
valid
, i.e. satisfied by any functor to a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC. Recall that
eqs.

9
and
10
are satisfied by virtue of every hom-set of a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC being a
\mathcal{V}
-relation (along with
proposition

2.8
).

•

The following implication is valid because sequential composition is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation morphism
eq.

3
.

f
=_{\varepsilon}
f^{\prime}
\Rightarrow
=_{\varepsilon+\delta}
,
g
=_{\delta}
g^{\prime}
f
g
f^{\prime}
g^{\prime}

(11)

•

The following implication is valid because parallel composition is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation morphism
eq.

4
.

f
=_{\varepsilon}
g
\Rightarrow
=_{\varepsilon+\delta}
,
f^{\prime}
=_{\delta}
g^{\prime}
f
f^{\prime}
g
g^{\prime}

(12)

•

The following implications are valid because equality is an equivalence relation congruent with respect to both compositions and to the
\mathcal{V}
-relations on homsets.

\begin{array}[]{c}\emptyset\Rightarrow f=f\qquad f=g\Rightarrow g=f\qquad f=g,g=h\Rightarrow f=h\\
f=f^{\prime},g=g^{\prime}\Rightarrow f\mathbin{;}g=f^{\prime}\mathbin{;}g^{\prime}\qquad f=f^{\prime},g=g^{\prime}\Rightarrow f\otimes g=f^{\prime}\otimes g^{\prime}\\
f=f^{\prime},f=_{\varepsilon}g\Rightarrow f^{\prime}=_{\varepsilon}g\qquad g=g^{\prime},f=_{\varepsilon}g\Rightarrow f=_{\varepsilon}g^{\prime}.\end{array}

(13)

•

The equations in
fig.

1
, seen as implications with no premises, are all valid because
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMCs satisfy the laws of SMCs.

We often omit writing
\emptyset
when a
\mathcal{V}
-implication has no premise.

Definition 3.3
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-theory

\mathscr{T}
consists of a monoidal signature
\Sigma
and a set of
\mathcal{V}
-implications over
\Sigma
called
axioms
. A
model
of
\mathscr{T}
is a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC equipped with a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
that satisfies all the implications in
\mathscr{T}
.

A theory can be closed under standard implicational reasoning to contain all additional implications that are satisfied by all its models. For example, for any (
\mathcal{V}
-)equation
\varphi
, all models trivially satisfy
\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi
, so that implication should be in the closure of
\mathscr{T}
. More generally, the closure is a consequence relation.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\vdash
denote a set of
\mathcal{V}
-implications and write
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi\in{\vdash}
infix as
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
. We say that
\vdash
is a
consequence relation
if the following holds.

(1)

\varphi\in\Gamma
implies
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

(2)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
and
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
implies
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\varphi
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Delta
and
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\psi
, then
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\psi
.

Moreover, as we said in
example

3.2
,
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
and
fig.

1
are always satisfied, so they must also be put in the closure of
\mathscr{T}
. Perhaps unsurprisingly, the laws of consequence relations,
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
, and
fig.

1
are necessary and sufficient to construct the set of
all

\mathcal{V}
-implications satisfied by all models of
\mathscr{T}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Given a theory
\mathscr{T}
, let
\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}
denote the smallest consequence relation containing
\mathscr{T}
and all the
\mathcal{V}
-implications in
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
and
fig.

1
.
We call it the
closure
of
\mathscr{T}
.

Remark 3.6
.

It can be more convenient to write a
\mathcal{V}
-implication as an inference rule:

\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\Rightarrow\varphi\qquad\rightsquigarrow\qquad\hbox{
\ignorespaces \ignorespaces \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 13.15277pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{$\varphi_{1}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{$...$}\hskip 1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{$\varphi_{n}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to69.58694pt{\hrule\hfill}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 30.52264pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}
\ignorespaces}.

Then, one can show that
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}\varphi
if and only if there is a proof tree with conclusion
\varphi
and inference rules corresponding to implications in
\mathscr{T}
,
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
, or
fig.

1
such that all leaves are in
\Gamma
or empty.

In order to prove our claim that
\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}
contains all implications satisfied by all models, we construct the initial model, or
syntactic category
,
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}
as follows.

•

Its objects are lists of sorts in
\Sigma_{0}
.

•

Its morphisms are terms in
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
quotiented by the relation
\equiv
defined by
f\equiv g\Leftrightarrow{\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}f=g}
.

•

The
\mathcal{V}
-relation
d^{\mathscr{T}}
on homsets is defined by
d^{\mathscr{T}}(f,g)=\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid{\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}f=_{\varepsilon}g}\right\}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Any model
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
of
\mathscr{T}
uniquely factorises through an enriched symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Theorem 3.8
.

For any
\mathcal{V}
-implication
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
,
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}\varphi
if and only if all models of
\mathscr{T}
satisfy
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
.

4
Axiomatisation of KL Divergence for Stochastic Matrices

The two monoidal theories we recalled in
figs.

2
and
3
allow us to reason about stochastic matrices using string diagrams. The goal of this section is to extend this reasoning with quantitative implications in order to reason diagrammatically about the KL divergence between stochastic matrices.

In
section

4.1
, we show that KL divergence is compatible with the categorical structures of stochastic matrices, yielding two
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMCs:
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
. In
section

4.2
, we extend the theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
with
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications to obtain an axiomatisation of
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
. In
section

4.3
, we similarly extend
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
to obtain an axiomatisation of
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
.

4.1
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMCs of Stochastic Matrices

KL divergence (
definition

2.9
) was defined on probability distributions, but we can extend it to stochastic matrices via a column-wise maximum. Indeed, seeing each column of an
m\times n
stochastic matrix as a distribution in
\mathcal{D}\underline{m}
sending
j
to the value of the
j
th entry of that column, we define
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
on
\mathsf{FStoch}(n,m)
by

\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A,B)\coloneq\max_{i\in\underline{n}}\mathsf{kl}(A_{i},B_{i}),

(14)

where
A_{i}
(resp.
B_{i}
) is the
i
th column of
A
(resp.
B
), and
\mathsf{kl}(A_{i},B_{i})
is the KL divergence between the corresponding distributions in
\mathcal{D}\underline{m}
. When
m
or
n
are zero, we set
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}([],[])\coloneq 0
. Perrone showed in
[
51
, Proposition 2.10]
that this definition satisfies both requirements (
eqs.

3
and
4
) to obtain a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
, which is
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
equipped with
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
on each of its homsets.

It readily follows that equipping
\mathsf{BStoch}
with
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
on its homsets yields another
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC that we write
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
. By viewing
2^{m}\times 2^{n}
stochastic matrices as functions taking bit strings in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and returning distributions in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}^{m})
, we can rewrite
eq.

14
as

\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A,B)=\max_{u\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathsf{kl}(A(u),B(u)).

(15)

Furthermore, we can state the chain rule in
lemma

2.11
when
Y=\mathbb{B}^{n}
with string diagrams since distributions in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
correspond, via the isomorphism
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
defined in
eq.

1
, to morphisms in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}(0,n)
. For any morphisms
f_{\mathtt{1}},f_{\mathtt{0}},g_{\mathtt{1}},g_{\mathtt{0}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}(0,n)
, and any
p,q\in[0,1]
, we have (omitting applications of
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
)

\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\scalebox{0.7}{{

.

eq.

∎

We can also consider the direct sum instead of the Kronecker product, and
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
still satisfies
eq.

4
:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A\oplus A^{\prime},B\oplus B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\mathsf{kl}((A\oplus A^{\prime})_{i},(B\oplus B^{\prime})_{i})

\displaystyle=\max\{\max_{i}\mathsf{kl}(A_{i},B_{i}),\max_{j}\mathsf{kl}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j})\}

\displaystyle=\max\{\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A,B),\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A,B)+\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}),

where the second step holds because KL divergence is not affected by elements that are not in the support of the distributions involved. We obtain a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
, which is
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
equipped with
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
on its homsets.
We record for later the stronger intermediate step in the derivation above: for any morphisms
f,g:n\rightarrow m
and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}:n^{\prime}\rightarrow m^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
, we have

(18)

Analogously to
eq.

16
, we can state the chain rule of
lemma

2.12
with string diagrams, except now distributions in
\mathcal{D}\underline{m}
correspond to terms in
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}(1,m)
. For any morphisms
f,g\in\mathsf{FStoch}(1,m)
,
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FStoch}(1,m^{\prime})
, and any
p,q\in[0,1]
, we have

\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\scalebox{0.7}{${\hbox to51.29pt{\vbox to31.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.95276pt\lower-15.8226pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 0 L -26.88 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{
{}{}{}}{}{

4.2
Axiomatisation of
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}

In this section, we provide a quantitative monoidal axiomatisation of
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
. We rely on the axiomatisation of
\mathsf{BStoch}
in
[
52
]
with the monoidal theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
recalled in
fig.

2
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be the signature of
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
. The theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
is defined with the same signature
\Sigma
and the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications as axioms.

•

For any axiom
f=g
in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=g
.

•

For any
f:n\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
.

•

For any
f_{\mathtt{1}},g_{\mathtt{1}}:0\rightarrow m
,
f_{\mathtt{0}},g_{\mathtt{0}}:0\rightarrow m
, and
p,q\in[0,1]
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
contains
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
.

•

For any
f_{\mathtt{1}},g_{\mathtt{1}}:n\rightarrow m
and
f_{\mathtt{0}},g_{\mathtt{0}}:n^{\prime}\rightarrow m
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
contains
\textsc{If}_{\max}
.

We write
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
for the syntactic
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC generated by
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
.

Our axiomatisation result amounts to showing that the functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
defined in
eq.

1
yields an isomorphism of enriched categories between
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
and
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
. First, we show
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
satisfies the axioms in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

The
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
are satisfied by
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
.

Proof 4.3
.

As the underlying SMC of
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
is
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
, the functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
still factors through an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}\cong\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
by
[
52
, Theorem 3.13]
. It follows that
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
whenever
f=g
is in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
, hence the implications in the first item of
definition

4.1
are satisfied. The KL divergence satisfies
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\varphi)=0
for any distribution
\varphi
, hence
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
is satisfied for any term
f
. The implications represented by the inference rules
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
and
\textsc{If}_{\max}
are satisfied by
eq.

16
and
eq.

17
respectively. Indeed, while
eq.

16
and
eq.

17
are strict equalities, the inference rules with equalities up to
\varepsilon
are still valid because the distances in the conclusions are monotone functions of the distances in the premises (recall
eq.

7
).

By
lemma

3.7
, we obtain an enriched symmetric monoidal functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}\rightarrow\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
. Note that this is simply the isomorphism in the axiomatisation result of
[
52
]
, but we confirmed it is enriched. In particular, the underlying SMCs of
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
and
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}
are
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
and
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
respectively. For the latter, it is by definition, and for the former, it suffices to note that the only axioms that derive equations between terms in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
come from
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
.

It remains to show that
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is locally an isometry. We first tackle the case of terms
0\rightarrow m
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
f,g:0\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
, if
\varepsilon=\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)
, then
\ \Rightarrow f=_{\varepsilon}g
is in the closure of
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
.

Proof 4.5
.

We proceed by induction on
m
. For
m=0
, the result is handled by the axiom
f=_{0}f
because there is a single morphism in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}(0,0)
whose interpretation is the empty matrix
[]
.

Let
f,g:0\rightarrow m+1
be two morphisms in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
. By reasoning solely within
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
, we can decompose
f
and
g
as follows, where
p
is the total weight that
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
assigns to all bit strings starting with
\mathtt{1}
,
q
is that for
\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
, and
f_{\mathtt{x}}
and
g_{\mathtt{x}}
correspond to the distributions conditioned on the first bit being
\mathtt{x}
.

Our induction hypothesis says that there is a derivation, for each
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}
, of
f_{\mathtt{x}}=_{\varepsilon_{\mathtt{x}}}g_{\mathtt{x}}
, where
\varepsilon_{\mathtt{x}}=\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket)
. By applying the
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
rule, we derive
f=_{\varepsilon}g
with
\varepsilon=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\varepsilon_{\mathtt{1}}+(1-p)\varepsilon_{\mathtt{0}}
. By
eq.

5
, we find
\varepsilon=\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)
where
\varphi=p\cdot\mathord{\lvert\mathtt{1}\rangle}\otimes\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{1}}\right\rrbracket+(1-p)\cdot\mathord{\lvert\mathtt{0}\rangle}\otimes\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{0}}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
, and similarly
\psi=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
. Thus, we have derived
f=_{\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)}g
as desired.

The rest of the proof follows the axiomatisation of total variation distance in
[
20
]
.

Theorem 4.6
.

The function
f\mapsto\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
is a bijective isometry
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}(n,m)\rightarrow\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\otimes}(n,m)
.

Proof 4.7
.

We already know it is a nonexpansive function because
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is an enriched functor. It is a bijection because
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is an isomorphism of the underlying SMCs,
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
and
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
. Thus, we only need to show that
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\geq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}(f,g)
for any two morphisms
f,g:n\rightarrow m
.

We proceed by induction on
n
. The base case
n=0
is handled by
lemma

4.4
.
Given,
f,g:n+1\rightarrow m
, we have the following equations by reasoning in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
(see e.g.
[
11
, Equation 17]
).

for
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}
. Our induction hypothesis says that
f_{\mathtt{x}}=_{\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket)}g_{\mathtt{x}}
is derivable for each
\mathtt{x}
, then the
\textsc{If}_{\max}
rule derives
f=_{\varepsilon}g
, where
\varepsilon=\max\left\{\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{1}}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{\mathtt{1}}\right\rrbracket),\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{0}}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{\mathtt{0}}\right\rrbracket)\right\}
, and finally,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:klmaxalt}}}{{=}}\max_{u\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket(u),\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket(u))=\max_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\max_{v\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket(\mathtt{x}v),\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket(\mathtt{x}v))=\max_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{\mathtt{x}}\right\rrbracket)=\varepsilon.

We conclude that
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\geq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}(f,g)
, hence that
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is locally an isometry on all of
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
.

4.3
Axiomatisation of
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}

In this section, we provide a quantitative monoidal axiomatisation of
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
. We closely follow the structure of
section

4.2
, relying now on the monoidal presentation of
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
in
[
24
]
.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\Sigma
be the signature of
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
. The theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
is defined with the same signature
\Sigma
and the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications as axioms.

•

For any axiom
f=g
in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=g
.

•

For any
f:n\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
.

•

For any
f,g:1\rightarrow n
,
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}:1\rightarrow m
, and
p,q\in[0,1]
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
contains
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
.

•

For any
f,g:n\rightarrow m
and

We write
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
for the syntactic
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC generated by
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
.

Our axiomatisation result amounts to showing that the functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
defined in
eq.

2
yields an isomorphism of enriched categories between
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
. First, we show
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
satisfies the axioms in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

The
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
are satisfied by
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
.

Proof 4.10
.

As in the proof of
lemma

4.2
,
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
factors through an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}\cong\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
by
[
24
, Theorem 3.14]
, and it follows that
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
whenever
f=g
is in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
. The implications
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
are satisfied because
\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\varphi)=0
for any
\varphi
. The implications
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
and
\textsc{Par}_{\max}
are satisfied by
eq.

19
and by
eq.

18
respectively (we also use
eq.

8
).

By
lemma

3.7
, we obtain an enriched symmetric monoidal functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}\rightarrow\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
. Again, this is simply the isomorphism in the axiomatisation result of
[
24
]
, which is enriched. In particular, the underlying SMCs of
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
are
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
respectively.

It remains to show that
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is locally an isometry. We first tackle the case of terms
1\rightarrow m
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For any
f,g:1\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
,
\ \Rightarrow f=_{\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)}g
is derivable in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
.

Proof 4.12
.

We proceed by induction on
m
. For
m=0
or
m=1
, the result is trivial because there is no morphism in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}(1,0)
and a single one in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}(1,1)
.

Let
f,g:1\rightarrow m
be two morphisms in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
. By reasoning solely within
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
, we can decompose
f
and
g
as follows, where
p
is first entry of
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
,
q
is that for
\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
, and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
correspond the columns consisting of the remaining entries after normalisation. (This is essentially
[
24
, Lemma 3.2]
written recursively.)

\scalebox{0.7}{${\hbox to37.07pt{\vbox to13.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.28297pt\lower-6.64444pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

\lxSVG@closescope
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

\lxSVG@closescope
{

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{46.91214pt}{-13.892pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}

Our induction hypothesis says that there is a proof of
f^{\prime}=_{\varepsilon}g^{\prime}
where
\varepsilon=\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f^{\prime}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g^{\prime}\right\rrbracket)
. By applying the
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
rule (and
\mathrm{id}_{1}=_{0}\mathrm{id}_{1}
), we get
f=_{\varepsilon}g
with
\varepsilon=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+(1-p)\varepsilon
. By
eq.

6
, we find
\varepsilon=\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)
where
\varphi=p\mathord{\lvert m\rangle}+(1-p)\left\llbracket f^{\prime}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
and
\psi=q\mathord{\lvert m\rangle}+(1-q)\left\llbracket g^{\prime}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
. We thus derived
f=_{\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)}g
.

The rest of the proof follows the axiomatisation of total variation distance in
[
41
]
.

Theorem 4.13
.

The function
f\mapsto\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
is a bijective isometry
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}(n,m)\rightarrow\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}(n,m)
.

Proof 4.14
.

We already know it is a nonexpansive function because
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is an enriched functor. It is a bijection because
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
is an isomorphism of the underlying SMCs,
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
. Thus, we only need to show that
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\geq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}(f,g)
for any
f,g:n\rightarrow m
. By
[
24
, Propositions 3.12 and 3.13]
, we have a decomposition
f=(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n})\mathbin{;}p_{m}^{n}
and
g=(g_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{n})\mathbin{;}p_{m}^{n}
,
where
f_{i}:1\rightarrow m
and
\left\llbracket f_{i}\right\rrbracket
is the
i
th column of
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
, similarly for
g
, and
p_{m}^{n}:nm\rightarrow m
. By
lemma

4.11
,
f_{i}=_{\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f_{i}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{i}\right\rrbracket)}g_{i}
is derivable in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
for each
i
, and
p_{m}^{n}=_{0}p_{m}^{n}
is derived by the second axiom in
definition

4.8
. Then, we can apply
eq.

11
and
\textsc{Par}_{\max}
, to derive
f=_{\varepsilon}g
with
\varepsilon=\max_{i\in\underline{n}}\mathsf{kl}(\left\llbracket f_{i}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g_{i}\right\rrbracket)+0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:klmax}}}{{=}}\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)
.
We conclude that the distance between
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
is below
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)
as desired.

5
Axiomatisation of Rényi Divergences

KL divergence is an instance of a family of generalised relative entropies called Rényi divergences
[
55
]
, which are also
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-relations on
\mathcal{D}X
. These satisfy chain rules similar to
lemmas

2.11
and
2.12
, which means that our axiomatisations in
section

4
can be generalised as well. In fact, the proofs barely change.

In
section

5.1
, we recall the definition of Rényi divergences between distributions, and their extensions to stochastic matrices yielding two
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMCs
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\otimes}
and
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\oplus}
. In
section

5.2
, we explain what changes to
section

4
lead to axiomatisations of these
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMCs.

5.1
Rényi Divergences

Definition 5.1
.

Given a parameter
0<\alpha<\infty
with
\alpha\neq 1
, the
Rényi divergence of order
\alpha
is a map
\mathcal{D}X\times\mathcal{D}X\rightarrow{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
defined by
\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi,\psi)\coloneq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log(\sum_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi(x)^{\alpha}}{\psi(x)^{\alpha-1}})
, where
\frac{x}{0}
is
0
when
x=0
and
\infty
otherwise and
\log(\infty)=\infty
. For
\alpha=0,1,\infty
, the Rényi divergence is defined as a limit, or equivalently with the following formulas.

\textsf{ré}_{0}(\varphi,\psi)=-\log(\sum_{x\in X,\varphi(x)>0}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\psi(x))\qquad\textsf{ré}_{1}(\varphi,\psi)=\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)\qquad\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi,\psi)=\sup_{x\in X}\log\frac{\varphi(x)}{\psi(x)}

The Rényi divergences also satisfy a chain rule (see e.g.
[
54
, Section 7.12]
). We state the specific variants that we will need (
cf.

lemmas

2.11
and
2.12
), omitting the case
\alpha=1
which is the KL divergence.

Lemma 5.2
.

Given
0\leq\alpha<\infty
with
\alpha\neq 1
and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}\times Y)
with
p\coloneq\varphi(\mathtt{1},Y)
and
q\coloneq\psi(\mathtt{1},Y)
,

\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi,\psi)=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\big(\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))}+\frac{(1-p)^{\alpha}}{(1-q)^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-))}\big).

(20)

For
\alpha=\infty
, we have

\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi,\psi)=\max\big\{\log\frac{p}{q}+\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-)),\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-))\big\}.

(21)

Lemma 5.3
.

Given
0\leq\alpha<\infty
with
\alpha\neq 1
,
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(X+Y)
,
p\coloneq\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)
and
q\coloneq\sum_{x\in X}\psi(x)
,

\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi,\psi)=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\big(\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})}+\frac{(1-p)^{\alpha}}{(1-q)^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y})}\big).

(22)

For
\alpha=\infty
, we have

\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi,\psi)=\max\big\{\log\frac{p}{q}+\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X}),\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y})\big\}.

(23)

In order to avoid writing the long formulas in
eqs.

20
,
21
,
22
, and
23
many times, we will use the following shorthand function
C_{\alpha}:[0,1]^{2}\times[0,\infty]^{2}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
.

C_{\alpha}(p,q,\varepsilon,\delta)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log(\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\varepsilon}+\frac{(1-p)^{\alpha}}{(1-q)^{\alpha-1}}e^{(\alpha-1)\delta})&0\leq\alpha<\infty,\alpha\neq 1\\
\max\{\log\frac{p}{q}+\varepsilon,\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+\delta\}&\alpha=\infty\end{cases}

(24)

This function describes how the divergence between two joint distributions depends on the divergences between their conditionals. Note that for any
\alpha
,
C_{\alpha}
is monotone in the third and fourth arguments. Namely, we have analogues to
eqs.

7
and
8
, which means we can transform the chain rules of
lemmas

5.2
and
5.3
into sound
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications in the next section.

5.2
Axiomatisations

For the rest of this section, we fix the parameter
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
with
\alpha\neq 1
. We extend the definition of Rényi divergence of order
\alpha
to stochastic matrices following
eq.

14
: given two
m\times n
stochastic matrices, let
\overline{\textsf{ré}}_{\alpha}(A,B)\coloneq\max_{i\in\underline{n}}\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(A_{i},B_{i})
.
Perrone showed in
[
51
, Proposition 2.18]
that this definition satisfies
eqs.

3
and
4
, making
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\otimes}
equipped with
\overline{\textsf{ré}}_{\alpha}
on its homsets into a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC. This in turn yields a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC
\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\otimes}
, that is axiomatised by the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-theory.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\Sigma
be the signature of
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
. The theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
is defined with the same signature
\Sigma
and the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications as axioms.

•

For any axiom
f=g
in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CC}}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=g
.

•

For any
f:n\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
.

•

For any
f_{\mathtt{1}},g_{\mathtt{1}}:0\rightarrow m
,
f_{\mathtt{0}},g_{\mathtt{0}}:0\rightarrow m
, and
p,q\in[0,1]
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
contains
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
.

•

For any
f_{\mathtt{1}},g_{\mathtt{1}}:n\rightarrow m
and
f_{\mathtt{0}},g_{\mathtt{0}}:n^{\prime}\rightarrow m
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
contains
\textsc{If}_{\max}
.

We write
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
for the syntactic
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC generated by
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
.

Theorem 5.5
.

The functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
defined in
eq.

1
is an enriched isomorphism of SMCs
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}\cong\mathsf{BStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\otimes}
.

Proof 5.6
.

The proof follows that of
section

4.2
. In particular,

•

soundness of the new
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
rule is justified by
eq.

20
(or
eq.

21
when
\alpha=\infty
) and monotonicity of
C_{\alpha}
,

•

soundness of
\textsc{If}_{\max}
is justified by the same proof as for
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
, and

•

the use of
eq.

5
in
lemma

4.4
is replaced by
eq.

20
(or
eq.

21
when
\alpha=\infty
).

We can argue as for
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{kl}}^{\oplus}
that
\mathsf{FStoch}^{\oplus}
equipped with
\overline{\textsf{ré}}_{\alpha}
on its homset is a
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC. We denote it by
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\oplus}
and show it is axiomatised by the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-theory.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\Sigma
be the signature of
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
. The theory
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
is defined with the same signature
\Sigma
and the following
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-implications as axioms.

•

For any axiom
f=g
in
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{CA}}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=g
.

•

For any
f:n\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
contains
\ \Rightarrow f=_{0}f
.

•

For any
f,g:1\rightarrow n
,
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}:1\rightarrow m
, and
p,q\in[0,1]
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
contains
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
.

•

For any
f,g:n\rightarrow m
and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}:n^{\prime}\rightarrow m^{\prime}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
contains
\textsc{Par}_{\max}
.

We write
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
for the syntactic
{[0,\infty]_{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-SMC generated by
\mathscr{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

The functor
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
defined in
eq.

2
is an enriched isomorphism of SMCs
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}\cong\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\textsf{ré}\alpha}^{\oplus}
.
