.

The proof follows that of
section

4.3
. In particular,

•

soundness of the new
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
rule is justified by
eq.

22
(or
eq.

23
when
\alpha=\infty
) and monotonicity of
C_{\alpha}
,

•

soundness of
\textsc{Par}_{\max}
is justified by the same proof as for
\overline{\mathsf{kl}}
,

•

and the use of
eq.

6
in
lemma

4.11
is replaced by
eq.

22
(or
eq.

23
when
\alpha=\infty
).

6
Conclusions

In this paper, we have provided axiomatisations of relative entropy as an enrichment of
\mathsf{FStoch}
, leveraging string diagrammatic reasoning and quantitative equations. This represents a new application of quantitative equational reasoning (​​
[
45
]
) to distances between probabilistic processes, addressing a notable gap in the literature where relative entropy had not previously been treated within this framework.

While our development takes place at the level of monoidal categories, the theories
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\oplus}
and
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\oplus}}
are based on cocartesian theories (​​
[
13
]
). Consequently, by considering them as presentations of the opposite (cartesian) category, they can be translated into quantitative algebraic theories following the correspondence described in
[
41
, Section 6]
. The resulting presentation is a theory of convex algebras equipped with two additional quantitative inferences, expressed in the style of
[
45
]
(without the metric axioms):

\vdash x=_{0}x\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=_{\varepsilon}x^{\prime},y=_{\delta}y^{\prime}\vdash x+_{p}y=_{C(p,q,\varepsilon,\delta)}x^{\prime}+_{q}y^{\prime},

where
C
is the function
C_{\alpha}
defined in
eq.

24
or
C_{1}(p,q,\varepsilon,\delta)=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\varepsilon+(1-p)\delta
. This may serve as a quantitative algebraic axiomatisation of relative entropy between probability distributions, rather than generic stochastic matrices as considered in the present paper.
The monads induced by these theories map a discrete space on a set
X
to
\mathcal{D}X
equipped with the corresponding relative entropy. Extending this description to non-discrete spaces remains a direction for future work.

By contrast, the theories
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{KL}\otimes}
and
\mathscr{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}^{\otimes}}
are neither cartesian nor cocartesian, and therefore require the abstraction of the monoidal setting. Their axiomatisations contribute to recent developments in categorical probability, where quantitative reasoning via enrichment is emerging, e.g. in
[
51
]
. In that work, divergence is treated abstractly and instantiated to specific relative entropies, yielding only a general formulation of the chain rule. Our rules
\textsc{Chain}_{\otimes}
and
\textsc{Chain}_{\oplus}
, in contrast, are sufficiently precise to characterise the corresponding relative entropies uniquely. This opens the possibility of synthetic proofs for results that depend on the specific properties of KL or Rényi divergences.

Another promising direction concerns the string diagrammatic study of Bayesian reasoning (​​
[
33
,
31
,
32
,
43
]
). KL divergence plays a central role in evaluating probabilistic learning models (​​
[
22
,
36
]
); our framework suggests the possibility of proving mathematical properties of model performances using quantitative diagrammatic reasoning.

The generalisation from quantitative equations to quantitative implications was essential to accommodate the enrichments introduced in
sections

4
and
5
using the chain rules. Nevertheless, the theoretical foundations of
\mathcal{V}
-theories merit further clarification. While models may be regarded as functors out of the syntactic category (
lemma

3.7
), the converse does not generally hold, resulting in an incomplete functorial semantics. Adapting the functorial semantics of implicational theories (​​
[
9
,
1
]
) to the setting of monoidal algebra remains a question to be explored in future work. We also conjecture that the comparison established in
[
41
, Section 6]
extends to
\mathcal{V}
-theories: cartesian
\mathcal{V}
-theories (equipped with natural copy and discard structure) should correspond to quantitative algebraic theories as developed in
[
45
]
.
Our treatment of implicational axioms also allows to accomodate the different choices of inference rules in
[
41
]
more directly. Namely, the choice can be baked in the theory (as axioms) instead of in the generation of the free model as was the case in
[
41
]
. This is strictly more general than
[
41
]
, and it encompasses the example in
[
20
]
, which provides an axiomatisation of the total variation distance on
\mathsf{BStoch}^{\otimes}
that does not fit within the framework of quantitative monoidal algebra developed in
[
41
]
.

Finally, relative entropies have quantum counterparts that are relevant in quantum information and computing (​​
[
66
]
). Given the widespread adoption of diagrammatic reasoning in quantum settings (​​
[
15
]
), extending our results to diagrammatic theories of quantum processes is a natural and promising direction. Notably, quantum relative entropy has already been studied categorically, using string diagrams, in the context of quantum natural language processing (​​
[
8
]
).

Appendix

A
Appendix to
section

2

The chain rules are standard properties of KL chain rule (see e.g.
[
54
, Theorem 2.15]
). Nevertheless, we reproduce the full proof for the specific instances we required.

{pf*}
Proof of
lemma

2.11
.
We have the following derivation where only the third step requires careful analysis around edge cases.

\displaystyle\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\log\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{1},y)\log\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{1},y)}{\psi(\mathtt{1},y)}+\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{0},y)\log\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{0},y)}{\psi(\mathtt{0},y)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{1},y)\log\frac{p\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}{q\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}+\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{0},y)\log\frac{(1-p)\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}{(1-q)\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{1},y)\!\left(\log\frac{p}{q}+\log\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}\right)+\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(\mathtt{0},y)\!\left(\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+\log\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}\right)

\displaystyle=p\log\frac{p}{q}+p\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)\log\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-)(y)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+(1-p)\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)\log\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-)(y)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))+(1-p)\mathsf{kl}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-)).

The only subtlety is the third step, where we use
\log(ab)=\log(a)+\log(b)
, which holds for
a,b>0
but requires care when a factor is zero. Writing the step for a generic
\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}
, we need to verify that

\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\log\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}=\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\log\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)}+\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\log\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)},

(
*
)

where
\varphi(\mathtt{1},Y)=p
,
\varphi(\mathtt{0},Y)=1-p
,
\psi(\mathtt{1},Y)=q
, and
\psi(\mathtt{0},Y)=1-q
.
Recall our conventions
\log\frac{a}{0}=\infty
for any
a
, and
0\cdot\infty=0
. We handle the cases where at least one denominator factor is zero.

•

If
\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)=0
, then
eq.

∗

becomes
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot\infty=\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot\infty+\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot a
, where
a\in[0,\infty]
. When
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=0
, we get
0
on both sides, and when
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)>0
, we get
\infty
on both sides.

•

If
\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)=0
, then
eq.

∗

becomes
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot\infty=\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot a+\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)\cdot\infty
, where
a\in[0,\infty]
. When
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=0
, we get
0
on both sides, and when
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)>0
, we get
\infty
on both sides.

•

If both denominators are positive, then the logarithms on both sides of
eq.

∗

are finite. If
\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)=0
, then
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=0
, so both sides of
eq.

∗

are zero. If
\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)>0
and
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)=0
, then we also have
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=0
, so both sides of
eq.

∗

are zero.

∎

{pf*}

Proof of
lemma

2.12
.
We have the following derivation.

\displaystyle\mathsf{kl}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in X+Y}\varphi(e)\log\frac{\varphi(e)}{\psi(e)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)\log\frac{\varphi(x)}{\psi(x)}+\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi(y)\log\frac{\varphi(y)}{\psi(y)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}p\,\varphi_{X}(x)\log\frac{p\,\varphi_{X}(x)}{q\,\psi_{X}(x)}+\sum_{y\in Y}(1-p)\,\varphi_{Y}(y)\log\frac{(1-p)\,\varphi_{Y}(y)}{(1-q)\,\psi_{Y}(y)}

\displaystyle=\scalebox{1}{$\sum_{x\in X}p\,\varphi_{X}(x)\!\left(\log\frac{p}{q}+\log\frac{\varphi_{X}(x)}{\psi_{X}(x)}\right)+\sum_{y\in Y}(1-p)\,\varphi_{Y}(y)\!\left(\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+\log\frac{\varphi_{Y}(y)}{\psi_{Y}(y)}\right)$}

\displaystyle=\scalebox{1}{$p\log\frac{p}{q}+p\sum_{x\in X}\varphi_{X}(x)\log\frac{\varphi_{X}(x)}{\psi_{X}(x)}+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+(1-p)\sum_{y\in Y}\varphi_{Y}(y)\log\frac{\varphi_{Y}(y)}{\psi_{Y}(y)}$}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})+p\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})+(1-p)\mathsf{kl}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y}).

As in the proof of
lemma

2.11
, the only subtlety is the use of
\log(ab)=\log(a)+\log(b)
when one of
a,b
may be
0
. This is handled by our conventions
\log\frac{a}{0}=\infty
and
0\cdot\infty=0
:

•

If
q=0
and
p>0
, then for any
x\in X
with
\varphi_{X}(x)>0
the summand
p\,\varphi_{X}(x)\log\frac{p\,\varphi_{X}(x)}{q\,\psi_{X}(x)}
is
\infty
, and the right-hand side of
eq.

6
is also
\infty
since
\mathsf{kl}(\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}p\\
1-p\end{bmatrix}$}$},\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scalebox{0.55}{$\begin{bmatrix}q\\
1-q\end{bmatrix}$}$})=\infty
.

•

The case
1-q=0
is analogous.

•

If
q\in(0,1)
, then the logarithms are finite whenever the corresponding numerators are positive, and if a numerator is
0
the whole summand is
0
by
0\cdot\infty=0
.

∎

B
Appendix to
section

3

{pf*}

Proof of
lemma

3.7
.
Recall that
\equiv
is a relation on morphisms in
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
defined by
f\equiv g\Leftrightarrow{\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}f=g}
. Namely, the terms
f
and
g
are equated whenever there is a proof of
f=g
using the inference rules in
\mathscr{T}
and
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
whose leaves are empty. It follows from
eq.

13
and the axioms of consequence relations that (1)
\equiv
is an equivalence relation congruent with respect to the structure of an SMC. Moreover, one can show by structural induction on proofs of
\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}f=g
that (2) for any model
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
of
\mathscr{T}
, and any terms
f,g:u\rightarrow v
,
f\equiv g
implies that
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket
.

Next,
d^{\mathscr{T}}
is the family of
\mathcal{V}
-relations on the homsets of
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
defined by
d^{\mathscr{T}}(f,g)=\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid{\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}f=_{\varepsilon}g}\right\}
. Namely,
d^{\mathscr{T}}(f,g)
is the largest
\varepsilon
for which there is a proof of
f=_{\varepsilon}g
using the inference rules in
\mathscr{T}
and
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
whose leaves are empty. It follows from
eq.

13
that (i)
d^{\mathscr{T}}
is congruent with
\equiv
and from
eqs.

11
and
12
that both compositions are nonexpansive with respect to
d^{\mathscr{T}}
. Moreover, one can show by structural induction on proofs that (ii) for any model
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
of
\mathscr{T}
, and any terms
f,g:u\rightarrow v
,
d^{\mathscr{T}}(f,g)\sqsupseteq d^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{C}}(\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)
.

By items (1), (i), and the axioms in
fig.

1
, the quotient of
\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
by
\equiv
equipped with the
\mathcal{V}
-relations
d^{\mathscr{T}}
is a well-defined
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-SMC, which is
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}
. By items (2) and (ii), any model
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket:\mathcal{M}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
can be uniquely factored through a
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Rel}
-enriched symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
.
∎

{pf*}

Proof of
theorem

3.8
.
The forward implication is easy, albeit tedious, to check. It only requires to verify that the laws of consequence relations are sound, which stems from implicational reasoning, and that the implications
eqs.

9
,
10
,
11
,
12
, and
13
are all satisfied in all models.

For the converse direction, assume the
\mathcal{V}
-implication
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
is satisfied by all models of
\mathscr{T}
. We wish to show
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}\varphi
. Let
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
be the theory consisting of the signature
\Sigma
and the axioms of
\mathscr{T}
along with the set of implications
\{\emptyset\Rightarrow\psi\mid\psi\in\Gamma\}
. By definition,
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
is a theory whose models are exactly the models of
\mathscr{T}
that also satisfy every (
\mathcal{V}
)-equation in
\Gamma
.

By
lemma

3.7
, the syntactic category
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}^{\prime}}
is a model of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
, and it is in particular a model of
\mathscr{T}
. As
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi
is satisfied by all models of
\mathscr{T}
by hypothesis, it is satisfied by
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, by the construction of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
, every (
\mathcal{V}
)-equation in
\Gamma
is satisfied in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}^{\prime}}
. Hence, the conclusion
\varphi
must also be satisfied in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathscr{T}^{\prime}}
, which means there is a derivation of
\vdash^{\mathscr{T}^{\prime}}\varphi
. This is a proof tree using the axioms of
\mathscr{T}
or
\Gamma
, which we can see as a derivation (in the original theory) of
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathscr{T}}\varphi
as desired.
∎

C
Appendix to
section

5

{pf*}

Proof of
lemma

5.2
.

Case
0\leq\alpha<\infty
,
\alpha\neq 1
.

Unfolding the definition of Rényi divergence on the product
\mathbb{B}\times Y
, we have

\displaystyle e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi,\psi)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\sum_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha-1}}.

Writing
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)
and
\psi(\mathtt{x},y)=\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)
with
\varphi(\mathtt{1},Y)=p
,
\varphi(\mathtt{0},Y)=1-p
,
\psi(\mathtt{1},Y)=q
, and
\psi(\mathtt{0},Y)=1-q
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\sum_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha-1}}\sum_{y\in Y}\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)^{\alpha}}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)^{\alpha-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha-1}}\cdot e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-))}

\displaystyle=\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}\,e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))}+\frac{(1-p)^{\alpha}}{(1-q)^{\alpha-1}}\,e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-))}.

Taking
\log
and dividing by
\alpha-1
on both sides yields
eq.

20
.
As in
lemma

2.11
, we cannot justify the factoring step when some denominator is
0
. These cases are handled by the conventions built into the definition of
\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}
:

•

If
\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)=0
and
\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)>0
, then
\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha-1}}=\infty
and there exists some
y\in Y
such that
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=0
and
\varphi(x,y)>0
, so
\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha-1}}=\infty
, and we conclude that both sides are
\infty
;

•

If
\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)=0
then both
\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)^{\alpha-1}}
and
\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)^{\alpha-1}}
will be zero for every
y\in Y
, so the corresponding term vanishes (in particular, the choice of the conditional distribution
\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)
is irrelevant).

Case
\alpha=\infty
.

By definition,
\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi,\psi)=\log\sup_{(\mathtt{x},y)\in\mathbb{B}\times Y}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)}
. Writing
\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)=\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)
and
\psi(\mathtt{x},y)=\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathtt{x},y)\in\mathbb{B}\times Y}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},y)}

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\sup_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)\,\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{B}}\frac{\varphi(\mathtt{x},Y)}{\psi(\mathtt{x},Y)}\sup_{y\in Y}\frac{\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}{\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{x},-)(y)}

\displaystyle=\max\!\Big\{\frac{p}{q}\,e^{\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{1},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{1},-))},\;\frac{1-p}{1-q}\,e^{\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\overline{\varphi}(\mathtt{0},-),\overline{\psi}(\mathtt{0},-))}\Big\}.

Taking
\log
on both sides yields
eq.

21
. The edge cases are analogous.
∎

{pf*}
Proof of
lemma

5.3
.

Case
0\leq\alpha<\infty
,
\alpha\neq 1
.

Unfolding the definition and splitting the sum over
X+Y
, we have

\displaystyle e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi,\psi)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in X+Y}\frac{\varphi(e)^{\alpha}}{\psi(e)^{\alpha-1}}=\sum_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi(x)^{\alpha}}{\psi(x)^{\alpha-1}}+\sum_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(y)^{\alpha-1}}.

For
x\in X
we have
\varphi(x)=p\,\varphi_{X}(x)
and
\psi(x)=q\,\psi_{X}(x)
, and for
y\in Y
we have
\varphi(y)=(1-p)\,\varphi_{Y}(y)
and
\psi(y)=(1-q)\,\psi_{Y}(y)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi(x)^{\alpha}}{\psi(x)^{\alpha-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}\sum_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi_{X}(x)^{\alpha}}{\psi_{X}(x)^{\alpha-1}}=\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q^{\alpha-1}}\,e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})},

and similarly

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(y)^{\alpha}}{\psi(y)^{\alpha-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-p)^{\alpha}}{(1-q)^{\alpha-1}}\,e^{(\alpha-1)\textsf{ré}_{\alpha}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y})}.

Substituting back, taking
\log
, and dividing by
\alpha-1
yields
eq.

22
.

Case
\alpha=\infty
.

By definition,
\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi,\psi)=\log\sup_{e\in X+Y}\frac{\varphi(e)}{\psi(e)}
. Splitting the supremum over
X+Y
,

\displaystyle\sup_{e\in X+Y}\frac{\varphi(e)}{\psi(e)}

\displaystyle=\max\!\Big\{\sup_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi(x)}{\psi(x)},\;\sup_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi(y)}{\psi(y)}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\max\!\Big\{\frac{p}{q}\sup_{x\in X}\frac{\varphi_{X}(x)}{\psi_{X}(x)},\;\frac{1-p}{1-q}\sup_{y\in Y}\frac{\varphi_{Y}(y)}{\psi_{Y}(y)}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\max\!\Big\{\frac{p}{q}\,e^{\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi_{X},\psi_{X})},\;\frac{1-p}{1-q}\,e^{\textsf{ré}_{\infty}(\varphi_{Y},\psi_{Y})}\Big\}.

Taking
\log
on both sides yields
eq.

23
.
∎
